## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**The series framing assumes an aeroplane, and this article has none.** Every canonical section that asks about sizing, systems, flight or flight data returns the same answer here, that the number never named a vehicle. **The framing is kept so that the X-52 sits in its place in the sequence, and the sections it imposes are short because the record is.**

**The framing of a decision with a recorded reason also breaks down.** The registry gives the reason as possible confusion with the B-52 series, but no document giving the reasoning has been seen, and whether that phrase quotes the decision or paraphrases it cannot be determined \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\]. The F-19 case shows that confusion can be stated as a reason when it is not the reason, so the stated ground is evidence of what was written and not necessarily of what was weighed.

**The argument for a spoken channel is borrowed rather than found.** The opening states that the radio is argued from the neighbouring cases and not from this one, and the C-16 note is the only entry in the family that names a radio. **The Epistemic State section below separates what the record establishes from what this article infers**, and the inference that the refusal lacked written authority stands on an absence in a document, weaker evidence than a presence would be.

## The Source Base

**The thirty curated sources that carry the argument and the 1,335 research records that map the field are different things, and this article keeps them apart on purpose.**

**The evidentiary base is small because the subject is.** Thirty curated references and four books carry every claim made here, and the ones that matter most are the five issues of the governing instruction that precede and follow the decision. **Those are not commentary on the evidence. They are the evidence**, because the central question is what a document does and does not contain, and the only way to settle that is to read it.

**The survey base maps the field.** Its 1,335 research records are drawn from the scholarly registry of digital object identifiers, Crossref, and from two report servers, the Defense Technical Information Center and the space agency's Technical Reports Server. **A few of them are cited in the prose**, being the flight test report of the aircraft that received X-53A, for that programme's dates, the three report-server studies of pilot-controller communication named under The Definition of Primary, and the titles named under Thin Subjects to show what the literature does and does not contain. **None is cited for a claim about the X-52 decision**, and the argument about the decision would stand unchanged without the survey.

**The research survey holds 1,335 research records drawn from Crossref, the Defense Technical Information Center and the NASA Technical Reports Server**, of which 30 are report primaries, being 2.2 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 2012.** Of the 1,280 dated records, 843 were published in 2006 or later, the year of the refusal and after it, being 65.9 percent.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the space agency's Technical Reports Server, the Defense Technical Information Center or the energy department's report server, or when it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports.

**The report-primary fraction is 2.2 percent, being 30 records of 1,335, which is the third-lowest of the 72 articles in this series that carry research references**, counting in each article the research definitions whose address meets that rule. **The lowest is the [X-39][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned], an anomaly without a vehicle, at 0.52 percent, being 4 records of 768, and that is not a coincidence.** The second-lowest is the later article on the seven numbers from X-69 to X-75, at 1.79 percent, being 22 records of 1,232, and its subject is likewise a run of designations that no aeroplane carries.

**The measure means less here than it does anywhere else in this series and the article will not dress it up.** It counts identifiers issued by the space agency's report server and the defence technical information centre, which is the right instrument when the subject is an aeroplane and the wrong one when the subject is a refusal. **The primary documents this article actually argues from are the five issues of the governing instruction, the registry that records the decision, the drug regulator's naming guidance and the civil call sign study**, and not one of them carries an identifier the measure can see.

**So the count is reported alongside the fraction.** **Eleven curated sources are primary documents rather than descriptions of one**, being five issues of the governing instruction, three of the drug regulator's naming publications, the civil aviation call sign study, the air-ground communications briefing note and the flight research centre's own fact sheet. **A number that falls because the subject changed is reporting something true**, and the X-39, the seven numbers from X-69 to X-75 and the X-52, whose subjects are administrative acts rather than aeroplanes, are the three at the bottom of the ranking.

**The report literature of the armed services does not hold this subject.** It holds aeronautical engineering, and the confusability of spoken identifiers is studied in medicine, in civil aviation safety and in linguistics. **The report literature that does bear on it is the space agency's own study of addressee errors in air traffic control communications**, which treats the call sign problem directly, with its neighbours on pilot-controller communication \[[Monan 1983][research_callsign_problem]\] \[[An error-resistant linguistic protocol for air traffic control][research_error_resistant_protocol]\] \[[Morrow et al. 1990][research_routine_pilot_controller]\].

### Thin Subjects

**Six subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| a threshold for deciding that two names are too alike | threshold, cutoff, decision rule, false positive, false negative, ROC, sensitivity and specificity, or a word beginning criteri, discriminat or validat | 9 |
| the longest common subsequence | longest common subsequence, LCS | 0 |
| the bigram set and the Dice coefficient | bigram, bigrams, Dice | 1 |
| aircraft designation as a subject | designation, designations, nomenclature together with aircraft, airplane, aeroplane, military, Air Force, Navy, Army | 2 |
| the reuse or retirement of an issued identifier | reuse, recycle, retire, reassign and their inflections together with identifier, number, numbering, name, code, designation | 0 |
| a collision between issued identifiers | collision, collisions, collide, colliding together with identifier, namespace, name, DOI, number | 3 |

**A threshold is the thing this literature most rarely names, and the evidence a threshold would rest on is a pharmacy literature.** Of the 9 titles that carry a threshold term, two concern the similarity of strings or sounds as such, being \[[Fischer 1982][research_fischer_1982]\] and \[[Austin 1957][research_austin_1957]\], and one the criteria of the likelihood of confusion in trademark law \[[Moon 2018][research_moon_2018]\]. **The term rule misses the closest work**, which is a small literature whose titles relate a measure of drug name similarity to experimental data or to observed errors \[[Lambert et al. 1999][research_lambert_lin_1999]\] \[[Lambert et al. 2002][research_lambert_donderi_2002]\] \[[Tamaki et al. 2012][research_tamaki_satoh_2012]\] \[[Lambert et al. 2015a][research_lambert_bhaumik_2015]\] \[[Sagawa et al. 2024][research_sagawa_kizaki_2024]\]. The drug regulator's guidance states that its screening threshold rests on the validation work done on its algorithms, and that in its postmarketing experience the combined similarity measure has been positively correlated with errors involving name confusion \[[Best Practices in Developing Proprietary Names for Human Prescription Drug Products, Guidance for Industry, December 2020][ref_fda_naming]\]. **The claim that a similarity threshold can rest on evidence therefore has a literature, and nothing comparable exists for designations.**

**Two of the three measures the article computes have almost no titles of their own.** No title names the longest common subsequence, and the one title that names a bigram concerns open-bigram coding of letters in recognition memory rather than a similarity coefficient.

**Aircraft designation is not a subject of this literature.** The two titles that pair designation or nomenclature with an aircraft or service term are standards naming aircraft hydraulic, pneumatic and oxygen equipment, and neither concerns a mission design series designation.

**The occupancy argument has no literature, and collision between issued identifiers has three titles.** No title concerns the reuse or retirement of an issued identifier, which is the rule on which the B-52's occupancy of the number 52 rests. The three titles on collision concern namespace collisions, collisions between author names, and colliding digital object identifiers.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject**, which is the refusal of an identifier because an identifier already in use is near it, together with what such a refusal draws on. That means the similarity of strings, names and spoken forms, confusable names and their screening, medication error where names, labels or reporting are concerned, the spoken and the written identifier, the administration of identifiers, the legal test of confusing similarity, human error, the perception of confusable sounds and words, and aviation's own designations and channels. **The survey therefore reaches into medicine, law, linguistics and psychology where a title concerns confusable names or identifiers**, because refusing a proposed drug name because an approved name is near it is the same administrative act as refusing X-52A because a B-52 exists. **The pharmacology is not the subject. The refusal is.**

**The survey has no residual cluster.** Every admitted record belongs to one of the ten named literatures in the table above, under the same rule.

**Several classes are excluded because this subject's words also name other things.** Readback names the signal read back from storage and from programmable logic, as well as the pilot's repetition of a clearance. Radiotelephony names a branch of radio engineering as well as a phraseology, and verbal communication names a social skill as well as a spoken exchange. A generic name is a rank in the naming of fungi and trematodes as well as the pharmacist's term for a nonproprietary drug name. Legibility names a social and political idea as well as a property of print. An identifier is also a molecular tag, a device fingerprint and a name in a program, a namespace is also a library of program classes, and a numbering system is also a numeral system, a notation for teeth and a term of textiles. Also excluded are confusion as a clinical symptom or a conceptual muddle, the brand name as a marketing or pricing variable, consumer confusion over sustainable products, machine recognition of speech, characters and images where the machine is the subject, similarity measures on graphs, trees, images, trajectories, music and molecules, the similarity of documents by meaning, exact pattern matching, orthography and phonetics studied as philology, medication error with no name, label or reporting element, glossary definitions of terms, drug handbook name lists, textbook appendices, correction notices and second deposits of one document. A review of a book, a notice such as front matter, an editorial or a news item, and a reference or definition entry of two pages or fewer are excluded on the same ground, since none of them is a research work in its own right.

**Doubtful classes are kept where the title concerns a confusable, spoken or issued identifier, and a reader may reasonably discount them.** They are consumer confusion studied as a marketing construct, because confusion between similar brands is one of its kinds, general medication error prevention, machine recognition of call signs and readbacks, numbering schemes for tools, alloys and engine positions, unique identifiers designed for data systems, and encyclopedia entries on string matching and record linkage.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a record is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

### The Literature of This Refusal Is Not a Literature of Aircraft Designation

**Of the 1,335 research records, 21, or 1.57 percent, sit in the aviation cluster, and not one of them is about aircraft designation as a subject in its own right.** Seven concern aviation safety reporting, ten controller-pilot data link communication, one cockpit speech intelligibility, one aircraft identification training, and two are flight reports on the aircraft that received X-53A. **The aviation literature the survey does hold is the literature of the radio channel**, being the 96 records of the call sign cluster, which concern phraseology, readback and miscommunication rather than designations. **The literature of why the X-52 was refused lies almost entirely outside aircraft designation**, and an article in a series about aeroplanes should report that rather than pad the row.

### The Book Identifiers Match Title and Author

**All four resolve to the books the labels name** \[[Cushing, Fatal words][book_cushing]\] \[[Reason, Human error][book_reason]\] \[[Bowker and Star, Sorting things out][book_bowker_star]\] \[[Norman, The psychology of everyday things][book_norman]\]. **The match is made in the repository's search index on title and author**, because its work endpoint returns the same internal error for records that exist and for keys that do not, so it cannot tell a wrong identifier from a dead one.

## Epistemic State

### Historical Fact

The designation X-52A was requested in 2006 for a programme to test active aeroelastic wing technology, was disapproved because of possible confusion with the B-52 series, and the designation X-53A was allocated to the programme instead. This is recorded by the specialist registry of United States military aerospace vehicle designations \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\].

The Active Aeroelastic Wing programme began in 1996, first flew in modified form in November 2002, and concluded its flight research in March 2005 \[[Active Aeroelastic Wing Flight Research, NASA Facts FS-2005-03-061 DFRC][ref_aaw_factsheet]\] \[[Clarke et al. 2005][research_aaw_flight_test]\] \[[Boeing X-53 Active Aeroelastic Wing][ref_x53_wikipedia]\]. The fact sheet gives the start of flight testing as late 2002 and the flight test report as November 2002.

The aircraft was designated X-53 by memorandum of the Air Force Deputy Chief of Staff for Strategic Plans and Programs, dated 16 August 2006 by the encyclopedia entry \[[Boeing X-53 Active Aeroelastic Wing][ref_x53_wikipedia]\], and the Air Force Research Laboratory announced the designation on 11 December 2006 \[[Active Aeroelastic Wing flight research vehicle receives X-53 designation, Air Force Research Laboratory, 11 December 2006][ref_afrl_x53_release]\].

The office symbol of the deciding office changed from HQ USAF/XPPE to AF/A8PE with effect from 1 February 2006 \[[Allocation of Official Aerospace Vehicle MDS Designations][ref_mds_allocation]\].

The F-35 designation was approved on 5 June 2002 against a nomenclature office recommendation of F-24A made on the ground that design numbers are to be assigned consecutively \[[Non-Standard DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_nonstandard_mds]\].

The requests to redesignate two drones as Q-7 and Q-8, made because the drones were being confused with unmodified aircraft, were refused in March 1954 \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\].

The Food and Drug Administration screens proposed prescription drug names against approved names using its Phonetic and Orthographic Computer Analysis program, makes that program publicly available, and publishes three similarity bands, being a combined match percentage score of 70 percent or more for a highly similar name pair, 55 to 69 percent for a moderately similar pair, and 54 percent or less for a low similarity pair. **The guidance states that the 55 percent screening threshold is set on the validation work done on the algorithms** \[[Best Practices in Developing Proprietary Names for Human Prescription Drug Products, Guidance for Industry, December 2020][ref_fda_naming]\] \[[Phonetic and Orthographic Computer Analysis Program][ref_fda_poca]\].

The United Kingdom's civil aviation authority published a dedicated call sign confusion study in April 2000, resting on the 482 call sign confusion reports of 1997, of which 435 were filed by controllers, 44 by flight crews and 3 by both \[[CAP 704, Aircraft Call Sign Confusion Evaluation Safety Study, United Kingdom Civil Aviation Authority][ref_cap704]\].

### Verified by Reading the Document

**The following are statements about the contents of five issues of one instruction and were checked against the text of each rather than by consulting a description of any of them.**

The 14 April 2005 issue of the joint instruction directs that the coordinating office will assign and reserve the next available consecutive design number within each basic mission for new vehicles \[[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]\].

That issue contains no authority to skip a design number, no occurrence of the word skip, and no criterion by which a proposed design number may be judged unsuitable.

Its duplication check, its trademark search and its four levels of review apply to the popular name and not to the designation.

Its only provision citing confusion in connection with a designator is the prohibition of the letters I and O as series symbols \[[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 14 April 2005][ref_afi_16_401_2005]\].

The 1 September 1997 issue of the same instruction and the 1994 issue it superseded carry the same sequence requirement, the same popular-name duplication check and trademark search, and the same prohibition of I and O, and likewise contain no authority to skip a design number \[[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 1 September 1997][ref_afi_16_401_1997]\] \[[Air Force Joint Instruction 16-401, 1994][ref_afi_16_401]\].

The 16 May 2014 issue adds, at Attachment 2 paragraph A2.1.6.1.2, that HAF/A8PE reserves the authority to skip design number at discretion and that skipped design numbers cannot be requested or used, and attaches no criterion to the authority \[[Air Force Instruction 16-401, 16 May 2014][ref_afi_16_401_2014]\]. The 3 November 2020 issue keeps the authority at the same paragraph under AF/A8PE, and at paragraph 4.2.1.3 it states that requests are not accepted for designators in reverse or skipped sequences \[[Department of the Air Force Instruction 16-401, 3 November 2020][ref_dafi_16_401_2020]\].

### Analysis

The edit distance between the strings X-52 and B-52 is 1, the normalised edit similarity is 0.75, the longest common subsequence is three characters giving the same 0.75, and the Dice coefficient on character bigrams is two thirds. These are computed and not quoted from any source.

**Rendered as they would be spoken, the two names are more similar than they are in writing.** The character edit distance between `ex fifty two` and `bee fifty two` is 2 over strings of 12 and 13 characters, giving a normalised similarity of about 0.846 against the written pair's 0.75. **At the level of whole words one token in three differs, giving about 0.667**, so the ordering depends on what a listener is taken to be matching.

The X-35 was allocated before the F-35 was approved, so that approval raised the number of series holding the design number 35 from one to two and added one bit of residual uncertainty to a listener who hears the number without the mission letter. The refusal of X-52A held the corresponding quantity at 52 unchanged and added none.

The B-52 has been operated by the Air Force since 1955 and is expected to serve into the 2050s, so the numeral has been and will be occupied for something near a century, and had been occupied for fifty-one years at the moment of the refusal \[[Boeing B-52 Stratofortress][ref_b52]\].

Every collision named in this article is between two designations that differ in the mission symbol and agree in the design number.

The count of cross-series numeric collisions in a system where each mission series counts from one is structurally non-zero and grows as series are added.

**Placing this article's own similarity figures in the drug regulator's published bands is an illustration and not a result.** The bigram coefficient of about 66.7 percent would fall in the moderately similar band and the normalised edit similarity of 75 percent in the highly similar band, but **neither figure is the combined orthographic and phonetic score that program computes**, and no claim is made that the program would return either value.

### Inference

**That the X-52A refusal was made under an authority the instruction did not then contain is an inference from the absence of any such provision in the 2005 issue**, and an absence in a document is weaker evidence than a presence. It is possible that the authority existed in another issuance not consulted here.

**That the 2014 clause regularised the practice is an inference from its content and its timing**, and no document has been seen that connects the two.

**That the F-35 and X-52 decisions are inconsistent is an interpretation.** The two were made four years apart under one office symbol and then another, and the individuals need not have been the same. **The inconsistency asserted here is of the system and not of any person.**

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**Who asked for X-52A is not stated in any source consulted.** The registry says the request was made for a programme to test active aeroelastic wing technology and does not name the requesting office.

**The date within 2006 is not stated**, so whether the request preceded or followed the 1 February office-symbol change is unknown.

**No document giving the reasoning has been seen.** The phrase possible confusion with the B-52 series is the registry's summary. Whether it quotes the decision or paraphrases it is not determinable from the public record.

**Whether the B-52 was named in the decision because of its fame, its longevity, or its role as the carrier aircraft for X-planes is unknown**, and the third possibility is the one this article finds most interesting and has the least evidence for.

**Whether anyone considered the F-35 precedent in 2006 is unknown.**

**Whether the refusal was correct is not settled by anything here.** No measure and no threshold were recorded, and this article supplies a measure only to show what was missing, not to supply the answer retrospectively.

## Out of Scope

**The Active Aeroelastic Wing itself.** The aeroelastic engineering, the control laws, the roll-reversal problem and the flight-test results belong to the next article in this series, which is about the X-53. **This article names the programme and stops.**

**The unmanned series consuming numbers from the same pool.** The X-58 and X-67 slots were taken by aircraft designated XQ-58A and XQ-67A, a different mechanism from a refusal, treated in its own articles later in the series \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\].

**The pre-1962 designation systems.** Everything here concerns the joint system implemented in September 1962 \[[1962 United States Tri-Service Aircraft Designation System][ref_tri_service_1962]\].

**Foreign designation systems**, and the question of whether they exhibit the same behaviour.

**The surveyed literature itself.** The survey is a map for a reader who wants to pursue the general question, and its records are listed by title. **None of them is cited for a claim about the X-52 decision.** The cluster commentary carries one citation and it is a curated source rather than a survey record, being the drug regulator's naming guidance, which is cited because the commentary makes a claim about what that regulator publishes.

## Conclusion

**The X-52 is the only number in the X-series that was asked for properly and refused on the merits of its own string.** The registry's other missing X numbers are the [X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23], requested in 1965 and refused because the vehicle was unmanned and then never requested again, the [X-39][related_post_a336_x39_reserved_never_assigned], reserved and never requested, and the X-58 and X-67, whose slots were skipped after aircraft designated in another series took the numbers \[[Missing USAF and DOD Aircraft Designations][ref_missing_mds]\].

**The instruction in force required the next number in sequence, provided no authority to skip one, and reserved its entire confusability apparatus for the popular name**, the half of the identifier it describes as aiding communications and media references rather than the half it calls official. **Its only written anti-confusion rule for a designator is that the series letters I and O are prohibited because they look like digits.**

**On the issues read here, the refusal was therefore made under a rule that did not exist in writing, and eight years later a sentence was added to the instruction granting the office the authority to skip a design number at discretion, with no criterion attached.** **The issue before the one in force said the same as the one in force, so the authority was absent for nearly twenty years while the practice ran throughout them.**

**The decision was not eccentric.** It sits in a long and documented family, and the reasons recorded across that family are operational rather than clerical. A radio under combat load. Maintenance records mixed up between two aircraft built by two companies both called Bell. **In every case the collision crosses a mission boundary, so the system's formal guarantee of uniqueness depends on a coordinate that the channel does not carry.**

**And the same office had spent 2002 manufacturing such a collision on purpose**, taking the X-35's number for the F-35 against its own nomenclature office's advice that numbers are assigned consecutively.

**A judgement of that kind can be written down, and elsewhere it is.** The drug regulator that screens every proposed prescription name against the names already approved publishes the program it uses, the databases it compares against, and three numbered similarity bands, and records that its screening threshold rests on validation work rather than on somebody's ear. **Measured against those bands, the two designations land in different categories depending on which measure is chosen**, and for that reason the choice of measure has to be stated. **One naming authority publishes all of it. The other refused a designation and recorded a phrase.**

**What the X-52 case finally shows is a designation system doing something a numbering scheme is not supposed to do.** A counter does not care what its numbers resemble. **This one does, the record of it weighing resemblance runs back to at least 1954, it has never written down what it is comparing or against what threshold, and in 2006 it looked at a bomber that had already been flying for fifty-one years and was expected to be flying more than forty years longer, and said no.**
